Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user (Director) is coordinating EMNLP 2026 paper revisions (DDL 5/25) through a Paper Agent role. This session handled:
   - **Page budget verification**: Ensuring main body ≤8 pages after Phase 1 comprehensive rewrite
   - **Phase 2 Statistical Data Integration**: Integrating Holm-Bonferroni corrected p-values, clustered bootstrap CIs (n_eff=119), gap statistic tier validation, and cross-dataset dual-direction transfer results
   - **§3.4 Decomposition downgrade**: label_only vs full_trace no longer significant under clustered bootstrap → all decomposition claims downgraded to "numerical trend"
   - **Cross-dataset results**: Bidirectional HE↔MBPP transfer data integrated with cascading updates across 6 sections
   - **Oscillation metrics**: Training dynamics quantitative data (CV 9.4×, convergence std, max drop 5.29pp)
   - **Minor fixes**: "18+pp" → "18–23pp", condition name consistency check
   - **Server sync**: Director noted server .tex files are stale; sync needed after all edits

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EMNLP 2026 8-page main body limit (references/limitations/appendix unlimited)
   - Clustered bootstrap with effective n=119 (not 607 due to 10 HumanEval variants per problem)
   - Holm-Bonferroni correction: 3/80 borderline within-tier pairs lost significance
   - Gap statistic analysis supporting k=2 tier clustering (silhouette ≥0.74)
   - "Numerical trend" language replacing "confounded upper-bound estimate" for decomposition
   - Bidirectional cross-dataset transfer: HE→MBPP 99.41%, MBPP→HE 100.0%
   - Oscillation metrics: eval accuracy CV (9.4× difference), loss rolling variance (46×)
   - Diagnostic signal dilution (not "information overload") as core terminology
   - Two-tier signal hierarchy (Tier 1: exceptions ≥98.53%, Tier 2: loop 77-86%)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/main.tex`**
     - Contains Abstract and Limitations — key cascade targets for all changes

     - Abstract line 23: "cross-dataset transfer (99.41%)" → "bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100%)"
     - Abstract line 24: Decomposition downgraded: "suggests ≥74%... though this trend does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap (n_eff=119)"
     - Limitations line 43: "yields a numerical trend that does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap, is limited to the Qwen family, and confounds multiple optimization dynamics"

   - **`experiments.tex`** (most heavily modified)
     - Line 6-7 footnote: Added "($n_\text{eff}{=}119$) are ${\sim}2.2\times$ wider" and "Holm-Bonferroni correction preserves all cross-tier comparisons; 3/80 borderline within-tier pairs lost significance."
     - Line ~102: Added "Gap statistic analysis independently supports $k{=}2$ clusters for 3/4 models and pooled data (silhouette ${\geq}$0.74)."
     - Lines 131-135 (cross-dataset paragraph, new):
       ```latex
       \paragraph{Cross-dataset transfer.}
       Bidirectional evaluation confirms generalization: HumanEval${\to}$MBPP \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 99.41\% ($N{=}4{,}270$) and MBPP${\to}$HumanEval achieves 100.0\% ($N{=}1{,}640$), while \texttt{no\_trace} drops to near-majority baseline in both directions (70.56\% vs.\ 69.02\% majority; 67.99\% vs.\ 61.65\%).
       The near-zero transfer loss for exception signals---across independently authored problem sets with no problem overlap---confirms SFT learns language-level diagnostic patterns, not dataset-specific artifacts.
       ```
     - Table 3 caption: "Label-only loss yields a +4.50pp numerical trend (not significant under clustered bootstrap, 95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp)"
     - §3.4 body (lines ~158-166, rewritten from 8→5 sentences):
       ```latex
       Label-only loss improves accuracy by +4.50pp (3-seed mean), a numerical trend suggesting gradient dilution accounts for at most ${\sim}$26\% of the gap; however, this difference does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap (95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\text{eff}{=}119$), and the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\sim}$1/500 tokens), regularization (NTP removed), and effective learning rate.
       The remaining ${\sim}$74\% is numerically attributed to diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but this attribution inherits the non-significance of the underlying difference.
       Directionally consistent evidence: tail-truncation at ${\sim}$500 tokens (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}) crosses the globals module dump threshold across all four models; Qwen-1.5B yields a similar pattern (17\%/83\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\%), limiting the decomposition to the Qwen family.
       Error analysis confirms the mechanism: all 217 \texttt{full\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through a mechanical truncation pathway rather than semantic confusion.
       The 26\%/74\% split is a numerical trend, not a statistically confirmed decomposition; controlled gradient masking ablations are needed to isolate individual confounders.
       ```

     - Removed "Caveats" paragraph (3 lines, redundant with Limitations)

bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms SFT acquires genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching.\nA preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family suggests ${\\geq}$74\\% of the degradation reflects diagnostic signal dilution, though this trend does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap ($n_\\text{eff}{=}119$) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate.\nA two-tier signal hierarchy holds across families, with a sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens, yielding a practical trace selection strategy: extract exception events first (Tier~1, ${\\geq}$98.53\\%), fall back to tail-truncated state otherwise (93--97\\%).\nFor SFT-based verdict prediction, the binding constraint is diagnostic signal density, not information quantity.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{method}\n\\input{experiments}\n\\input{discussion}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations.\nFirst, all experiments use HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized (591 problems, simple assert-based tests on short Python functions); generalization to human-written code, other languages, non-assert test frameworks (e.g., parametric tests, integration tests, flaky tests), and multi-file projects is unverified.\nSecond, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; models at 30B+ parameters may have sufficient capacity to overcome diagnostic signal dilution, and general-purpose LLMs may behave differently.\nThird, multi-seed experiments use only 3 seeds (the minimum for variance estimation); larger seed pools would strengthen robustness claims.\nFourth, \\texttt{full\\_trace} was not evaluated on DebugBench due to the supplementary nature of that analysis; whether complete traces degrade on human-written buggy code remains untested.\nFifth, the decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:density}) yields a numerical trend that does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap, is limited to the Qwen family, and confounds multiple optimization dynamics; future work should isolate individual factors through controlled gradient masking ablations.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.\nAll models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.\nTraining uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on enterprise-grade GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.\nTotal compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\\times$ multiple conditions $\\times$ multiple seeds $\\times$ iterative development).\nWe do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}\nThe results suggest execution traces contain two independent signal pathways for verdict prediction, whose interaction explains why complete traces degrade SFT learning.\nWe present this as a descriptive working hypothesis, not a causal model.\n\n\\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}\nThe diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% under \\texttt{exception\\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).\nAlthough 55.5\\% of failures are silent logic errors, \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% fail recall because test assertions generate \\texttt{AssertionError} events.\n\\texttt{ae\\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.\n\n\\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}\nTail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\\approx} 0$.\nTail truncation at ${\\geq}$512 tokens captures \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding ${\\sim}$73\\% ($\\approx$ \\texttt{no\\_trace}).\nThe +13.8pp phase transition at tail-384$\\to$tail-512 marks where the model accesses terminal execution state.\n\n\\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}\nBoth pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\\,=\\,0 by step 200), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence (final std\\,=\\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\\times$ (0.022 vs.\\ 0.002), with a maximum single-step drop of 5.29pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}\n\\caption{Training dynamics: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges to 99.83\\% by step 150; \\texttt{full\\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence.}\n\\label{fig:training_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) exceeds the best regex (B5, 99.33\\%) by 0.50pp on caught-exception boundary cases (McNemar $p{=}.014$), provides calibrated probabilities (ECE\\,=\\,0.0027; \\S\\ref{sec:calibration}), and retains 97.65\\% under marker removal vs.\\ B5's 28.49\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\nBidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) with near-majority \\texttt{no\\_trace} performance confirms the learned patterns are language-level, not dataset-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{The marginal value of complete traces.}\nSource code alone achieves 73--76\\% (\\texttt{no\\_trace}), so traces add at most ${\\sim}$27pp; complete traces capture only 4--9pp of this headroom while exception events capture 24--27pp.\nThat more information degrades performance aligns with feature selection theory \\citep{guyon2003introduction}, the Information Bottleneck \\citep{tishby2000information}, and LIMA \\citep{zhou2023lima}.\n\n\\paragraph{Design implications.}\nThe two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state when no exception is raised.\nTier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9/+7.2pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.\n\n",
        "numLines": 41,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 41
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nExecution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \\citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.\nThe intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.\nYet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.\nComplete traces \\emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.\n\nPrior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \\citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.\nVerdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.\nThis approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \\citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.\n\nWe present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.\nUsing 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \\citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \\citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \\citep{deepseek2024coder}.\n\nThe central result: complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models and seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\nA simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \\emph{why complete traces hurt}.\nMarker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\nA preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) yields a numerical trend: gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% of the gap, with diagnostic signal dilution (the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload'') at ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nTraining dynamics confirm this: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\\% without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nA tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\\geq}$98.53\\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: a numerical trend via label-only loss on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) suggests gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% and diagnostic signal dilution ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 33,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 33
      }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0182tG9Vyr1pfzYVpq5XSRVB`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete execution traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) while compact exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.33\%; bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) and marker-removal robustness (97.65\% vs.\ regex collapse to 28.49\%) confirm SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns.
4	A two-tier signal hierarchy yields a practical trace selection strategy: exception events first, tail-truncated state (93--97\%) otherwise; a preliminary decomposition suggests ${\geq}$74\% reflects diagnostic signal dilution, though this trend does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap ($n_\text{eff}{=}119$).
5	These findings suggest prioritizing diagnostic density over completeness in trace-based tool design; controlled gradient masking ablations and generalization to other languages, non-assert tests, and larger models remain as future work.
6	
```

    "newString": "These findings suggest prioritizing diagnostic density over completeness in trace-based tool design; gradient masking ablations and broader generalization remain as future work.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nAcross four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete execution traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while compact exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\%; bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) and marker-removal robustness (97.65\\% vs.\\ regex collapse to 28.49\\%) confirm SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns.\nA two-tier signal hierarchy yields a practical trace selection strategy: exception events first, tail-truncated state (93--97\\%) otherwise; a preliminary decomposition suggests ${\\geq}$74\\% reflects diagnostic signal dilution, though this trend does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap ($n_\\text{eff}{=}119$).\nThese findings suggest prioritizing diagnostic density over completeness in trace-based tool design; controlled gradient masking ablations and generalization to other languages, non-assert tests, and larger models remain as future work.\n",

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate across two research questions:
4	(1)~Why do complete traces degrade SFT verdict accuracy despite containing all diagnostic information?
5	(2)~Which trace components carry the verdict signal, and how does their density affect learning?
6	All experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\% CIs.\footnote{Problem-level partition (472 train / 119 test, zero crossover; \S\ref{sec:dataset}). \texttt{exception\_only} duplicates (48.99\% test; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}) arise from within-problem variants; deduplication yields 0.00pp delta. Problem-level clustered bootstrap CIs ($n_\text{eff}{=}119$) are ${\sim}2.2\times$ wider but all Tier~1 vs.\ Tier~2 gaps (${\geq}$12pp) remain significant ($p{<}.001$). Holm-Bonferroni correction preserves all cross-tier comparisons; 3/80 borderline within-tier pairs lost significance.}
7	Pairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ unless noted.
8	
9	\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}
10	\label{sec:cross_model}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning an 8.7$\times$ parameter range (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families under key conditions; single-seed entries are marked with $^\dag$.
13	Regex B5 (99.33\%) exploits the \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker, providing a non-learned baseline; its collapse to 28.49\% under marker removal (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}) confirms the SFT advantage is not format-driven.
14	
15	\begin{table}[t]
16	\centering
17	\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\pm$std over 2--3 seeds; $^\dag$ = single seed (seed 42). Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}.}
18	\label{tab:cross_model}
19	\small
20	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
21	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}

47	\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}
48	Under \texttt{exception\_only}, all four models achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% across all seeds: Qwen-7B (99.83\%$\pm$0.00, 3 seeds), Qwen-1.5B (99.83\%), CodeLlama-13B (99.89\%$\pm$0.10, 3 seeds; seed 123 achieves 100\%), and DeepSeek-6.7B (99.39\%$\pm$0.10, 3 seeds).

50	
51	\paragraph{Exception signals across model scales.}
52	Tier~1 signals are scale-invariant across 1.5B--13B (8.7$\times$): \texttt{exception\_only} spans 0.50pp (99.33--99.89\%) and \texttt{ae\_only} 1.18pp (98.74--99.92\%).
53	CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\%, confirming the 2 persistent errors (\texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts; 99.83\% is a floor, not a ceiling.
54	Scale effects concentrate in Tier~2: \texttt{loop\_only} spans 8.57pp (77.06--85.63\%), with CodeLlama-13B below Qwen-1.5B, suggesting architecture matters more than parameter count for lower-density signals.
55	In contrast, \texttt{no\_trace} scales with capacity (72.35--76.47\%, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_scale}) and shows the highest seed variance ($\sigma{=}$4.48pp).
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}
60	\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\ model scale. \texttt{exception\_only} maintains ${\geq}$99.33\% regardless of scale, while \texttt{no\_trace} shows monotonic sensitivity (+4.12pp). \texttt{nomarker} (markers removed) shows no scale degradation.}
61	\label{fig:cross_scale}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}
65	Excluding outlier seeds, VOTA achieves ${\geq}$98.57\% across all four models (0.80pp range).
66	DeepSeek seed 42 (84.45\%) is a tokenizer-driven truncation failure (91.7\% of samples exceed 2{,}048 tokens after tokenization; Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), demonstrating that VOTA's longer sequences are sensitive to tokenizer efficiency: 1/4 models suffers catastrophic truncation under standard training length. \texttt{exception\_only}'s compact inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep this vulnerability entirely.
67	
68	\subsection{Component Ablation}
69	\label{sec:ablation}
70	
71	Multi-seed \texttt{ae\_only} ablations use Qwen-7B; \texttt{loop\_only} is evaluated across three models to test Tier~2 generalization (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
72	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy validated across all four models and three architecture families (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}): Tier~1 (${\geq}$98.53\%) and Tier~2 (77--86\%) are separated by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs.
73	
74	\begin{table}[t]
75	\centering
76	\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\dag$/$^\S$/$^\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\pm$std; unmarked entries are single seed (seed 42). Per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}
77	\label{tab:ablation}
78	\small
79	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
80	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
81	\toprule
82	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Components} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Tier} \\
83	\midrule
84	ae\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\
85	exception\_only & exception events & \textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\
86	VOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\
87	VOTA\_no\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\
88	loop\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\
89	full\_trace & complete trace & 81.44{\scriptsize$\pm$1.08} & --- & --- \\
90	no\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\
91	\midrule
92	Regex (best) & exc.\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\
93	3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\
94	Zero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\
95	\bottomrule
96	\end{tabular}%
97	}
98	\end{table}
99	
100	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%$\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\%$\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
101	Removing assertion points has no effect (\texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae} = 99.41\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.
102	The Tier~1/2 boundary is unambiguous: the lowest Tier~1 condition (\texttt{ae\_only} on DeepSeek, 98.53\%$\pm$0.30, 2 seeds) exceeds the highest Tier~2 (\texttt{loop\_only} on Qwen-7B, 86.05\%, CI [83.97, 87.96]) by 12.48\,pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.
103	Gap statistic analysis independently supports $k{=}2$ clusters for 3/4 models and pooled data (silhouette ${\geq}$0.74).
104	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} lifts over \texttt{no\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen shows +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B, $p{<}.0001$), with the larger 7B gain likely reflecting greater capacity to exploit structured loop patterns; DeepSeek +6.95pp (80.31\%), CodeLlama +0.93pp (77.40\%, n.s.).
105	\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}
106	The strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}.001$).
107	The gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
108	Across all formats, SFT outperforms the best applicable regex by +3.62--23.86pp (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
109	
110	\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}
111	Zero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\% across four models (42.44pp range); three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\%) improves only 1.76pp.
112	SFT compresses this range to 0.50pp (99.39--99.89\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).
113	\paragraph{Frontier model crossover.}
114	Two frontier models confirm that SFT's advantage depends on input density.
115	GPT-4o zero-shot achieves 87.98\% on \texttt{exception\_only} and 84.45\% on \texttt{no\_trace}; GPT-4.1 achieves 82.18\% and 77.31\%, respectively.
116	Under \texttt{no\_trace}, both frontier models outperform SFT Qwen-7B (75.13\%) by +9.32pp (GPT-4o) and +2.18pp (GPT-4.1).
117	Under \texttt{exception\_only}, the ranking reverses: SFT Qwen-7B (99.83\%) exceeds GPT-4o by 11.85pp and GPT-4.1 by 17.65pp.
118	This crossover shows that compact diagnostic input unlocks SFT's full advantage, while absent or verbose traces favor general reasoning capacity.
119	
120	\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}
121	\label{sec:nomarker}
122	
123	The \texttt{exception\_only} format includes a \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\% accuracy; Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
124	To quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\texttt{nomarker} condition).
125	
126	Qwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\% $\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%).
127	Qwen-1.5B: 97.82\%, drop of 2.01pp.
128	The marker contribution (${\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\sim}$9\% of the total exception lift over \texttt{no\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).
129	Regex baselines confirm this decomposition: B5 (\texttt{[No exception data]}$\to$pass, 99.33\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\% without markers, confirming it is entirely format-dependent.
130	The strongest semantic regex B4 (exception class matching, 93.61\%) is marker-invariant; SFT \texttt{nomarker} (97.65\%) exceeds it by 4.04pp, isolating the learned semantic contribution beyond pattern matching.
131	
132	\paragraph{Cross-dataset transfer.}
133	Bidirectional evaluation confirms generalization: HumanEval${\to}$MBPP \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 99.41\% ($N{=}4{,}270$) and MBPP${\to}$HumanEval achieves 100.0\% ($N{=}1{,}640$), while \texttt{no\_trace} drops to near-majority baseline in both directions (70.56\% vs.\ 69.02\% majority; 67.99\% vs.\ 61.65\%).
134	The near-zero transfer loss for exception signals---across independently authored problem sets with no problem overlap---confirms SFT learns language-level diagnostic patterns, not dataset-specific artifacts.
135	
136	\subsection{Preliminary Decomposition (Confounded Upper-Bound Estimate)}
137	\label{sec:density}
138	
139	The 17.61pp gap between \texttt{full\_trace} (81.44\%$\pm$1.08, 3-seed mean) and VOTA (99.05\%, 3-seed mean) can be probed via \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only} (85.94\%$\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.
140	
141	\begin{table}[t]
142	\centering
143	\caption{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace gap (Qwen-7B, 3-seed means). Label-only loss yields a +4.50pp numerical trend (not significant under clustered bootstrap, 95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp); the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\sim}$1/500 tokens receive loss), implicit regularization (NTP on trace tokens removed), and effective learning rate.}
144	\label{tab:density}
145	\small
146	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
147	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
148	\toprule
149	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ full\_trace} & \textbf{Gap explained} \\
150	\midrule
151	full\_trace & 81.44{\scriptsize$\pm$1.08} & --- & --- \\
152	label\_only & 85.94{\scriptsize$\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp & 26\% \\
153	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & +17.61pp & 100\% \\
154	\bottomrule
155	\end{tabular}%
156	}
157	\end{table}
158	
159	Label-only loss improves accuracy by +4.50pp (3-seed mean), a numerical trend suggesting gradient dilution accounts for at most ${\sim}$26\% of the gap; however, this difference does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap (95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\text{eff}{=}119$), and the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\sim}$1/500 tokens), regularization (NTP removed), and effective learning rate.
160	The remaining ${\sim}$74\% is numerically attributed to diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but this attribution inherits the non-significance of the underlying difference.
161	Directionally consistent evidence: tail-truncation at ${\sim}$500 tokens (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}) crosses the globals module dump threshold across all four models; Qwen-1.5B yields a similar pattern (17\%/83\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\%), limiting the decomposition to the Qwen family.
162	Error analysis confirms the mechanism: all 217 \texttt{full\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through a mechanical truncation pathway rather than semantic confusion.
163	The 26\%/74\% split is a numerical trend, not a statistically confirmed decomposition; controlled gradient masking ablations are needed to isolate individual confounders.
164	
165	\subsection{Additional Analyses}
166	\label{sec:additional}
167	
168	\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}
169	\label{sec:trace_length}
170	Varying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\%--81.8\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\%, $p{=}.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).
171	This non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).
172	
173	\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}
174	Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).
175	All four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.
176	
177	A cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a sharp performance jump localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.
178	At tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\%, only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\to$512 (+13.2--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\sim}$94--98\%).
179	This localizes the critical threshold to ${\sim}$400--500 tokens, crossing the globals module dump threshold (${\sim}$384 tokens) and exposing structured execution markers (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).
180	CodeLlama tail conditions use label-only loss (\S\ref{sec:density}); cross-model tail comparisons should account for this training difference.
181	Beyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\%, 98.24\%] (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.
182	\texttt{tail\_2048} captures 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.
183	The residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).
184	
185	\paragraph{Calibration.}
186	\label{sec:calibration}
187	Calibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \texttt{exception\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\ 0.171 for \texttt{no\_trace}, a 63$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:calibration}).
188	This calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \texttt{exception\_only} (Table~\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.
189	
190	\paragraph{Error analysis.}
191	\texttt{exception\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown confirms all standard exception types achieve 100\% accuracy, with only \texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\ref{sec:exception_type}).
192	
193	\paragraph{Length control.}
194	Padding \texttt{exception\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\%), confirming the 18--23pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.
195	
196	\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}
197	\label{sec:debugbench}
198	On DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} 100\%, VOTA 99.77\%, \texttt{no\_trace} 96.71\%.
199	However, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \texttt{no\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis confirms the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).
200	
```

the lowest Tier~1 condition (\\texttt{ae\\_only} on DeepSeek, 98.53\\%$\\pm$0.30, 2 seeds) exceeds the highest Tier~2 (\\texttt{loop\\_only} on Qwen-7B, 86.05\\%, CI [83.97, 87.96]) by 12.48\\,pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.\nGap statistic analysis independently supports $k{=}2$ clusters for 3/4 models and pooled data (silhouette ${\\geq}$0.74).\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} lifts over \\texttt{no\\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen shows +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B, $p{<}.0001$), with the larger 7B gain likely reflecting greater capacity to exploit structured loop patterns; DeepSeek +6.95pp (80.31\\%), CodeLlama +0.93pp (77.40\\%, n.s.).\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross all formats, SFT outperforms the best applicable regex by +3.62--23.86pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range); three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp.\nSFT compresses this range to 0.50pp (99.39--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\\paragraph{Frontier model crossover.}\nTwo frontier models confirm that SFT's advantage depends on input density.\nGPT-4o zero-shot achieves 87.98\\% on \\texttt{exception\\_only} and 84.45\\% on \\texttt{no\\_trace}; GPT-4.1 achieves 82.18\\% and 77.31\\%, respectively.\nUnder \\texttt{no\\_trace}, both frontier models outperform SFT Qwen-7B (75.13\\%) by +9.32pp (GPT-4o) and +2.18pp (GPT-4.1).\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, the ranking reverses: SFT Qwen-7B (99.83\\%) exceeds GPT-4o by 11.85pp and GPT-4.1 by 17.65pp.\nThis crossover shows that compact diagnostic input unlocks SFT's full advantage, while absent or verbose traces favor general reasoning capacity.\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format includes a \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\\texttt{nomarker} condition).\n\nQwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\\% $\\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%).\nQwen-1.5B: 97.82\\%, drop of 2.01pp.\nThe marker contribution (${\\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).\nRegex baselines confirm this decomposition: B5 (\\texttt{[No exception data]}$\\to$pass, 99.33\\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\\% without markers, confirming it is entirely format-dependent.\nThe strongest semantic regex B4 (exception class matching, 93.61\\%) is marker-invariant; SFT \\texttt{nomarker} (97.65\\%) exceeds it by 4.04pp, isolating the learned semantic contribution beyond pattern matching.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset transfer.}\nBidirectional evaluation confirms generalization: HumanEval${\\to}$MBPP \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 99.41\\% ($N{=}4{,}270$) and MBPP${\\to}$HumanEval achieves 100.0\\% ($N{=}1{,}640$), while \\texttt{no\\_trace} drops to near-majority baseline in both directions (70.56\\% vs.\\ 69.02\\% majority; 67.99\\% vs.\\ 61.65\\%).\nThe near-zero transfer loss for exception signals---across independently authored problem sets with no problem overlap---confirms SFT learns language-level diagnostic patterns, not dataset-specific artifacts.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Decomposition (Confounded Upper-Bound Estimate)}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.61pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.44\\%$\\pm$1.08, 3-seed mean) and VOTA (99.05\\%, 3-seed mean) can be probed via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} (85.94\\%$\\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace gap (Qwen-7B, 3-seed means). Label-only loss yields a +4.50pp numerical trend (not significant under clustered bootstrap, 95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp); the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\\sim}$1/500 tokens receive loss), implicit regularization (NTP on trace tokens removed), and effective learning rate.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.44{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.08} & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.61pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nLabel-only loss improves accuracy by +4.50pp (3-seed mean), a numerical trend suggesting gradient dilution accounts for at most ${\\sim}$26\\% of the gap; however, this difference does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\\text{eff}{=}119$), and the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\\sim}$1/500 tokens), regularization (NTP removed), and effective learning rate.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$74\\% is numerically attributed to diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but this attribution inherits the non-significance of the underlying difference.\nDirectionally consistent evidence: tail-truncation at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}) crosses the globals module dump threshold across all four models; Qwen-1.5B yields a similar pattern (17\\%/83\\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\\%), limiting the decomposition to the Qwen family.\nError analysis confirms the mechanism: all 217 \\texttt{full\\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through a mechanical truncation pathway rather than semantic confusion.\nThe 26\\%/74\\% split is a numerical trend, not a statistically confirmed decomposition; controlled gradient masking ablations are needed to isolate individual confounders.\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\n\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a sharp performance jump localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\\%, only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512 (+13.2--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\sim}$94--98\\%).\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, crossing the globals module dump threshold (${\\sim}$384 tokens) and exposing structured execution markers (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\nCodeLlama tail conditions use label-only loss (\\S\\ref{sec:density}); cross-model tail comparisons should account for this training difference.\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%] (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\n\\texttt{tail\\_2048} captures 95--98\\% of \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\nThe residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.\n\n\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown confirms all standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy, with only \\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Length control.}\nPadding \\texttt{exception\\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\\%), confirming the 18--23pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.\n\n\\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%.\nHowever, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \\texttt{no\\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis confirms the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",

      "totalLines": 200
    }

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete execution traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) while compact exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.33\%; bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) and marker-removal robustness (97.65\% vs.\ regex collapse to 28.49\%) confirm SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns.
4	A two-tier signal hierarchy yields a practical trace selection strategy: exception events first, tail-truncated state (93--97\%) otherwise; a preliminary decomposition suggests ${\geq}$74\% reflects diagnostic signal dilution, though this trend does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap ($n_\text{eff}{=}119$).
5	These findings suggest prioritizing diagnostic density over completeness in trace-based tool design; gradient masking ablations and broader generalization remain as future work.
6	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012jWFsHuJtgWNE7pcFWx4EV`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.
4	The intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.
5	Yet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.
6	Complete traces \emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.
7	
8	Prior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.
9	Verdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.
10	This approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.
11	
12	We present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.
13	Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \citep{deepseek2024coder}.
14	
15	The central result: complete traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) while exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% across all models and seeds (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
16	A simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \emph{why complete traces hurt}.
17	Marker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\% while SFT retains 97.65\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
18	
19	A preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) yields a numerical trend: gradient dilution ${\leq}$26\% of the gap, with diagnostic signal dilution (the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload'') at ${\geq}$74\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\S\ref{sec:density}).
20	Training dynamics confirm this: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\% without stable convergence (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
21	A tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
22	
23	Our contributions:
24	\begin{enumerate}
25	\item \textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) while exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
26	
27	\item \textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\% while SFT retains 97.65\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
28	
29	\item \textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\geq}$98.53\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
30	
31	\item \textbf{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: a numerical trend via label-only loss on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) suggests gradient dilution ${\leq}$26\% and diagnostic signal dilution ${\geq}$74\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\S\ref{sec:density}).
32	\end{enumerate}
33	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}
5	The results suggest execution traces contain two independent signal pathways for verdict prediction, whose interaction explains why complete traces degrade SFT learning.
6	We present this as a descriptive working hypothesis, not a causal model.
7	
8	\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}
9	The diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% under \texttt{exception\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).
10	Although 55.5\% of failures are silent logic errors, \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 100\% fail recall because test assertions generate \texttt{AssertionError} events.
11	\texttt{ae\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.
12	
13	\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}
14	Tail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\% accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\approx} 0$.
15	Tail truncation at ${\geq}$512 tokens captures \texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding ${\sim}$73\% ($\approx$ \texttt{no\_trace}).
16	The +13.8pp phase transition at tail-384$\to$tail-512 marks where the model accesses terminal execution state.
17	
18	\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}
19	Both pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.
20	Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\,=\,0 by step 200), while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates (82--89\%) without convergence (final std\,=\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\times$ (0.022 vs.\ 0.002), with a maximum single-step drop of 5.29pp (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
21	
22	\begin{figure}[t]
23	\centering
24	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}
25	\caption{Training dynamics: \texttt{exception\_only} converges to 99.83\% by step 150; \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\%) without convergence.}
26	\label{fig:training_dynamics}
27	\end{figure}
28	
29	\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}
30	SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) exceeds the best regex (B5, 99.33\%) by 0.50pp on caught-exception boundary cases (McNemar $p{=}.014$), provides calibrated probabilities (ECE\,=\,0.0027; \S\ref{sec:calibration}), and retains 97.65\% under marker removal vs.\ B5's 28.49\% (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
31	Bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) with near-majority \texttt{no\_trace} performance confirms the learned patterns are language-level, not dataset-specific.
32	
33	\paragraph{The marginal value of complete traces.}
34	Source code alone achieves 73--76\% (\texttt{no\_trace}), so traces add at most ${\sim}$27pp; complete traces capture only 4--9pp of this headroom while exception events capture 24--27pp.
35	That more information degrades performance aligns with feature selection theory \citep{guyon2003introduction}, the Information Bottleneck \citep{tishby2000information}, and LIMA \citep{zhou2023lima}.
36	
37	\paragraph{Design implications.}
38	The two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state when no exception is raised.
39	Tier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9/+7.2pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.
40	
41	
```

1	\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}

14	\title{Complete Execution Traces Degrade SFT-Based Verdict Prediction: \\A Diagnostic Signal Analysis}
15	\author{Anonymous ACL submission}
16	
17	\begin{document}
18	\maketitle
19	
20	\begin{abstract}
21	Complete execution traces contain all information needed for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), yet they actively impair SFT learning.
22	Across four models spanning three architecture families (1.5B--13B parameters), complete traces achieve only 77--82\%, barely improving over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) and trailing compact exception events (${\geq}$99.33\%) by 18--23pp.
23	A regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\% while SFT retains 97.65\%; bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) confirms SFT acquires genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching.
24	A preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family suggests ${\geq}$74\% of the degradation reflects diagnostic signal dilution, though this trend does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap ($n_\text{eff}{=}119$) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate.
25	A two-tier signal hierarchy holds across families, with a sharp performance transition at ${\sim}$512 tail tokens, yielding a practical trace selection strategy: extract exception events first (Tier~1, ${\geq}$98.53\%), fall back to tail-truncated state otherwise (93--97\%).
26	For SFT-based verdict prediction, the binding constraint is diagnostic signal density, not information quantity.
27	\end{abstract}
28	
29	\input{introduction}
30	\input{method}
31	\input{experiments}
32	\input{discussion}
33	\input{related_work}
34	\input{conclusion}
35	
36	\section*{Limitations}
37	
38	Our study has several limitations.
39	First, all experiments use HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized (591 problems, simple assert-based tests on short Python functions); generalization to human-written code, other languages, non-assert test frameworks (e.g., parametric tests, integration tests, flaky tests), and multi-file projects is unverified.
40	Second, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; models at 30B+ parameters may have sufficient capacity to overcome diagnostic signal dilution, and general-purpose LLMs may behave differently.
41	Third, multi-seed experiments use only 3 seeds (the minimum for variance estimation); larger seed pools would strengthen robustness claims.
42	Fourth, \texttt{full\_trace} was not evaluated on DebugBench due to the supplementary nature of that analysis; whether complete traces degrade on human-written buggy code remains untested.
43	Fifth, the decomposition (\S\ref{sec:density}) yields a numerical trend that does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap, is limited to the Qwen family, and confounds multiple optimization dynamics; future work should isolate individual factors through controlled gradient masking ablations.
44	
45	\section*{Ethics Statement}
46	
47	This work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.
48	All models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.
49	Training uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on enterprise-grade GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.
50	Total compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\times$ multiple conditions $\times$ multiple seeds $\times$ iterative development).
51	We do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.
52	
53	\bibliography{references}
54	
55	\appendix
56	\input{appendix}
57	
58	\end{document}
59	
```

Label-only loss yields a +4.50pp numerical trend (not significant under clustered bootstrap, 95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp); the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\\sim}$1/500 tokens receive loss), implicit regularization (NTP on trace tokens removed), and effective learning rate.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.44{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.08} & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.61pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nLabel-only loss improves accuracy by +4.50pp (3-seed mean), a numerical trend suggesting gradient dilution accounts for at most ${\\sim}$26\\% of the gap; however, this difference does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\\text{eff}{=}119$), and the ablation simultaneously changes gradient selection (${\\sim}$1/500 tokens), regularization (NTP removed), and effective learning rate.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$74\\% is numerically attributed to diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but this attribution inherits the non-significance of the underlying difference.\nDirectionally consistent evidence: tail-truncation at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}) crosses the globals module dump threshold across all four models; Qwen-1.5B yields a similar pattern (17\\%/83\\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\\%), limiting the decomposition to the Qwen family.\nError analysis confirms the mechanism: all 217 \\texttt{full\\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through a mechanical truncation pathway rather than semantic confusion.\nThe 26\\%/74\\% split is a numerical trend, not a statistically confirmed decomposition; controlled gradient masking ablations are needed to isolate individual confounders.",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Decomposing the Full-Trace Degradation}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 18.07pp gap between \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) and \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) could reflect gradient dilution (diagnostic tokens are ${\\sim}$1/500 of full traces, diluting gradient signal during training) or content quality (verbose input buries diagnostic signals at inference).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled gradient masking.}\nTo isolate gradient dilution, we mask 99.8\\% of gradient updates during \\texttt{exception\\_only} training, matching the diagnostic token ratio of full traces while preserving compact input.\nThis yields 98.99\\% ($-$0.84pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only}), limiting gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the 18.07pp gap (Table~\\ref{tab:density}).\nContent quality---diagnostic signal density at inference---accounts for the remaining ${\\geq}$95\\%.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Decomposition of the full-trace degradation (Qwen-7B). Gradient masking isolates gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\%; content quality (signal density at inference) accounts for ${\\geq}$95\\%. Label-only loss provides a confounded but directionally consistent complementary estimate.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & 99.83{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & --- \\\\\n\\quad + grad mask (99.8\\%) & 98.99 & $-$0.84pp \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76$^\\ddag$ & $-$18.07pp \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{Complementary (confounded):}} \\\\\nfull\\_trace + label-only loss & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp$^\\dag$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\n$^\\ddag$Matched-seed baseline; 3-seed mean 81.44\\%$\\pm$1.08 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n$^\\dag$vs.\\ \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.44\\%$\\pm$1.08); not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\\text{eff}{=}119$).\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Complementary evidence.}\nLabel-only loss on full traces (+4.50pp, 3 seeds) yields a confounded upper bound of ${\\leq}$26\\% gradient contribution, consistent with the masking estimate (${\\leq}$5\\%) but not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp).\nQwen-1.5B shows a similar label-only pattern (17\\%/83\\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\\%), limiting this complementary analysis to the Qwen family.\nError analysis confirms the content quality mechanism: all 217 \\texttt{full\\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through mechanical truncation rather than semantic confusion."
}

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n% \\usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])\n\\usepackage{times,latexsym}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{algorithm}\n\\usepackage{algorithmic}\n\n\\title{Complete Execution Traces Degrade SFT-Based Verdict Prediction: \\\\A Diagnostic Signal Analysis}\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nComplete execution traces contain all information needed for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), yet they actively impair SFT learning.\nAcross four models spanning three architecture families (1.5B--13B parameters), complete traces achieve only 77--82\\%, barely improving over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) and trailing compact exception events (${\\geq}$99.33\\%) by 18--23pp.\nA regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\%; bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms SFT acquires genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching.\nA preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family suggests ${\\geq}$74\\% of the degradation reflects diagnostic signal dilution, though this trend does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap ($n_\\text{eff}{=}119$) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate.\nA two-tier signal hierarchy holds across families, with a sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens, yielding a practical trace selection strategy: extract exception events first (Tier~1, ${\\geq}$98.53\\%), fall back to tail-truncated state otherwise (93--97\\%).\nFor SFT-based verdict prediction, the binding constraint is diagnostic signal density, not information quantity.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{method}\n\\input{experiments}\n\\input{discussion}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations.\nFirst, all experiments use HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized (591 problems, simple assert-based tests on short Python functions); generalization to human-written code, other languages, non-assert test frameworks (e.g., parametric tests, integration tests, flaky tests), and multi-file projects is unverified.\nSecond, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; models at 30B+ parameters may have sufficient capacity to overcome diagnostic signal dilution, and general-purpose LLMs may behave differently.\nThird, multi-seed experiments use only 3 seeds (the minimum for variance estimation); larger seed pools would strengthen robustness claims.\nFourth, \\texttt{full\\_trace} was not evaluated on DebugBench due to the supplementary nature of that analysis; whether complete traces degrade on human-written buggy code remains untested.\nFifth, the decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:density}) yields a numerical trend that does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap, is limited to the Qwen family, and confounds multiple optimization dynamics; future work should isolate individual factors through controlled gradient masking ablations.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.\nAll models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.\nTraining uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on enterprise-grade GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.\nTotal compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\\times$ multiple conditions $\\times$ multiple seeds $\\times$ iterative development).\nWe do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
