    "oldString": "A preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) yields a numerical trend: gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% of the gap, with diagnostic signal dilution (the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload'') at ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
    "newString": "A controlled gradient masking ablation limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the degradation; content quality---diagnostic signal density at inference, the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload''---accounts for ${\\geq}$95\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nExecution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \\citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.\nThe intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.\nYet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.\nComplete traces \\emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.\n\nPrior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \\citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.\nVerdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.\nThis approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \\citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.\n\nWe present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.\nUsing 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \\citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \\citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \\citep{deepseek2024coder}.\n\nThe central result: complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models and seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\nA simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \\emph{why complete traces hurt}.\nMarker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\nA preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) yields a numerical trend: gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% of the gap, with diagnostic signal dilution (the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload'') at ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nTraining dynamics confirm this: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\\% without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nA tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\\geq}$98.53\\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: a numerical trend via label-only loss on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) suggests gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% and diagnostic signal dilution ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "oldString": "\\item \\textbf{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: a numerical trend via label-only loss on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) suggests gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% and diagnostic signal dilution ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Controlled decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: gradient masking on \\texttt{exception\\_only} ($-$0.84pp under 99.8\\% mask) limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the 18pp gap; content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference) is the dominant factor at ${\\geq}$95\\%. A complementary label-only ablation on the Qwen family yields a directionally consistent but confounded upper bound (${\\leq}$26\\%) (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nExecution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \\citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.\nThe intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.\nYet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.\nComplete traces \\emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.\n\nPrior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \\citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.\nVerdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.\nThis approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \\citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.\n\nWe present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.\nUsing 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \\citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \\citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \\citep{deepseek2024coder}.\n\nThe central result: complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models and seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\nA simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \\emph{why complete traces hurt}.\nMarker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\nA controlled gradient masking ablation limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the degradation; content quality---diagnostic signal density at inference, the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload''---accounts for ${\\geq}$95\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nTraining dynamics confirm this: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\\% without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nA tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\\geq}$98.53\\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Preliminary decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: a numerical trend via label-only loss on the Qwen family (7B and 1.5B, 3 seeds each) suggests gradient dilution ${\\leq}$26\\% and diagnostic signal dilution ${\\geq}$74\\%, though this difference does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp) and the ablation simultaneously alters gradient selection, regularization, and effective learning rate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}\nThe results suggest execution traces contain two independent signal pathways for verdict prediction, whose interaction explains why complete traces degrade SFT learning.\nWe present this as a descriptive working hypothesis, not a causal model.\n\n\\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}\nThe diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% under \\texttt{exception\\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).\nAlthough 55.5\\% of failures are silent logic errors, \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% fail recall because test assertions generate \\texttt{AssertionError} events.\n\\texttt{ae\\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.\n\n\\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}\nTail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\\approx} 0$.\nTail truncation at ${\\geq}$512 tokens captures \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding ${\\sim}$73\\% ($\\approx$ \\texttt{no\\_trace}).\nThe +13.8pp phase transition at tail-384$\\to$tail-512 marks where the model accesses terminal execution state.\n\n\\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}\nBoth pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\\,=\\,0 by step 200), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence (final std\\,=\\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\\times$ (0.022 vs.\\ 0.002), with a maximum single-step drop of 5.29pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}\n\\caption{Training dynamics: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges to 99.83\\% by step 150; \\texttt{full\\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence.}\n\\label{fig:training_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) exceeds the best regex (B5, 99.33\\%) by 0.50pp on caught-exception boundary cases (McNemar $p{=}.014$), provides calibrated probabilities (ECE\\,=\\,0.0027; \\S\\ref{sec:calibration}), and retains 97.65\\% under marker removal vs.\\ B5's 28.49\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\nBidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) with near-majority \\texttt{no\\_trace} performance confirms the learned patterns are language-level, not dataset-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{The marginal value of complete traces.}\nSource code alone achieves 73--76\\% (\\texttt{no\\_trace}), so traces add at most ${\\sim}$27pp; complete traces capture only 4--9pp of this headroom while exception events capture 24--27pp.\nThat more information degrades performance aligns with feature selection theory \\citep{guyon2003introduction}, the Information Bottleneck \\citep{tishby2000information}, and LIMA \\citep{zhou2023lima}.\n\n\\paragraph{Design implications.}\nThe two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state when no exception is raised.\nTier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9/+7.2pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.\n\n",

    "oldString": "Fifth, the decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:density}) yields a numerical trend that does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap, is limited to the Qwen family, and confounds multiple optimization dynamics; future work should isolate individual factors through controlled gradient masking ablations.",
    "newString": "Fifth, the gradient masking ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:density}) uses a single model (Qwen-7B) and seed; the complementary label-only analysis is limited to the Qwen family and does not reach significance under clustered bootstrap.",
    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n% \\usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])\n\\usepackage{times,latexsym}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{algorithm}\n\\usepackage{algorithmic}\n\n\\title{Complete Execution Traces Degrade SFT-Based Verdict Prediction: \\\\A Diagnostic Signal Analysis}\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nComplete execution traces contain all information needed for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), yet they actively impair SFT learning.\nAcross four models spanning three architecture families (1.5B--13B parameters), complete traces achieve only 77--82\\%, barely improving over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) and trailing compact exception events (${\\geq}$99.33\\%) by 18--23pp.\nA regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\%; bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms SFT acquires genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching.\nA controlled gradient masking ablation isolates content quality as the dominant factor: masking 99.8\\% of gradients during \\texttt{exception\\_only} training causes only $-$0.84pp (${\\leq}$5\\% of the 18pp gap), confirming diagnostic signal density at inference, not gradient dilution, is the binding constraint.\nA two-tier signal hierarchy holds across families, with a sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens, yielding a practical trace selection strategy: extract exception events first (Tier~1, ${\\geq}$98.53\\%), fall back to tail-truncated state otherwise (93--97\\%).\nFor SFT-based verdict prediction, the binding constraint is diagnostic signal density, not information quantity.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{method}\n\\input{experiments}\n\\input{discussion}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations.\nFirst, all experiments use HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized (591 problems, simple assert-based tests on short Python functions); generalization to human-written code, other languages, non-assert test frameworks (e.g., parametric tests, integration tests, flaky tests), and multi-file projects is unverified.\nSecond, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; models at 30B+ parameters may have sufficient capacity to overcome diagnostic signal dilution, and general-purpose LLMs may behave differently.\nThird, multi-seed experiments use only 3 seeds (the minimum for variance estimation); larger seed pools would strengthen robustness claims.\nFourth, \\texttt{full\\_trace} was not evaluated on DebugBench due to the supplementary nature of that analysis; whether complete traces degrade on human-written buggy code remains untested.\nFifth, the decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:density}) yields a numerical trend that does not reach significance under problem-level clustered bootstrap, is limited to the Qwen family, and confounds multiple optimization dynamics; future work should isolate individual factors through controlled gradient masking ablations.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.\nAll models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.\nTraining uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on enterprise-grade GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.\nTotal compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\\times$ multiple conditions $\\times$ multiple seeds $\\times$ iterative development).\nWe do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

Complete traces only capture 4-9pp of that potential gain, while exception events capture 24-27pp—suggesting that more information doesn't necessarily improve performance.

This aligns with established theory around feature selection and the Information Bottleneck principle. I could trim the LIMA citation to save space.

For the "Design implications" section, the two-tier hierarchy offers a practical approach: prioritize exception events first, then fall back to tail-truncated state when no exception occurs. The tier-2 benefit varies significantly by model—Qwen sees substantial gains while CodeLlama sees minimal improvement—so trace design should be tailored to the deployment model's capacity.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}\nThe results suggest execution traces contain two independent signal pathways for verdict prediction, whose interaction explains why complete traces degrade SFT learning.\nWe present this as a descriptive working hypothesis, not a causal model.\n\n\\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}\nThe diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% under \\texttt{exception\\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).\nAlthough 55.5\\% of failures are silent logic errors, \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% fail recall because test assertions generate \\texttt{AssertionError} events.\n\\texttt{ae\\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.\n\n\\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}\nTail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\\approx} 0$.\nTail truncation at ${\\geq}$512 tokens captures \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding ${\\sim}$73\\% ($\\approx$ \\texttt{no\\_trace}).\nThe +13.8pp phase transition at tail-384$\\to$tail-512 marks where the model accesses terminal execution state.\n\n\\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}\nBoth pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.\nControlled gradient masking quantifies this: masking 99.8\\% of gradients on \\texttt{exception\\_only} causes only $-$0.84pp, limiting gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the 18pp gap and confirming content quality at inference---not gradient dynamics---as the binding constraint (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\\,=\\,0 by step 200), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence (final std\\,=\\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\\times$ (0.022 vs.\\ 0.002), with a maximum single-step drop of 5.29pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}\n\\caption{Training dynamics: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges to 99.83\\% by step 150; \\texttt{full\\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence.}\n\\label{fig:training_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) exceeds the best regex (B5, 99.33\\%) by 0.50pp on caught-exception boundary cases (McNemar $p{=}.014$), provides calibrated probabilities (ECE\\,=\\,0.0027; \\S\\ref{sec:calibration}), and retains 97.65\\% under marker removal vs.\\ B5's 28.49\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\nBidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) with near-majority \\texttt{no\\_trace} performance confirms the learned patterns are language-level, not dataset-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{The marginal value of complete traces.}\nSource code alone achieves 73--76\\% (\\texttt{no\\_trace}), so traces add at most ${\\sim}$27pp; complete traces capture only 4--9pp of this headroom while exception events capture 24--27pp.\nThat more information degrades performance aligns with feature selection theory \\citep{guyon2003introduction}, the Information Bottleneck \\citep{tishby2000information}, and LIMA \\citep{zhou2023lima}.\n\n\\paragraph{Design implications.}\nThe two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state when no exception is raised.\nTier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9/+7.2pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.\n\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          "+Controlled gradient masking confirms this: $-$0.84pp under 99.8\\% mask limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the gap (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
          " Figure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\\,=\\,0 by step 200), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence (final std\\,=\\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\\times$ (0.022 vs.\\ 0.002), with a maximum single-step drop of 5.29pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).",
          " ",

```text
44	The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \texttt{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
45	
46	\subsection{Loss Ablation}
47	
48	To probe the contribution of gradient dilution, we introduce \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
49	TRL's SFTTrainer computes mean cross-entropy over label tokens, so label-only loss does not amplify the effective learning rate.
50	However, this ablation simultaneously changes three variables: (1)~which tokens receive gradient updates, (2)~the implicit regularization from predicting trace tokens, and (3)~the training signal distribution.
51	The resulting improvement over standard \texttt{full\_trace} provides an \emph{upper-bound estimate} of gradient dilution's contribution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but the decomposition is approximate (\S\ref{sec:density}).
52	
```

</details>

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "214f3b39-f0be-4ff3-a37b-452aff22096c",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "The resulting improvement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} provides an \\emph{upper-bound estimate} of gradient dilution's contribution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but the decomposition is approximate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
    "newString": "The resulting improvement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} provides a confounded upper bound on gradient dilution's contribution.\nA cleaner test---masking 99.8\\% of gradient updates during \\texttt{exception\\_only} training---matches the diagnostic token ratio of full traces while preserving compact input, isolating gradient dilution from content quality (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition and Diagnostic Signal Metrics}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\texttt{pass} or \\texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks.\n\nTo quantify signal quality without circular reference to model accuracy, we define the \\emph{diagnostic token ratio} $d = n_{\\text{diag}} / n_{\\text{total}}$, where $n_{\\text{diag}}$ counts tokens matching exception or assertion patterns.\nThis model-free metric correlates with accuracy at Pearson $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$, $N{=}5$ main conditions), but captures only the \\emph{explicit} diagnostic pathway; tail-truncated conditions achieve 93--97\\% with $d {\\approx} 0$, indicating a second, implicit signal channel operating through terminal execution state (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\texttt{no\\_trace}: Source code and test input only.\n\\item \\texttt{full\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA} (Verdict-Optimized Trace Abstraction): Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\n\\item \\texttt{exception\\_only}: Only exception/error events.\n\\item \\texttt{ae\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.\n\\item \\texttt{loop\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.44\\%), VOTA (99.05\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$); multi-seed means where available (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}). Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_examples} provides input/output examples.}\n\\label{fig:trace_overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2024coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\texttt{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo probe the contribution of gradient dilution, we introduce \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nTRL's SFTTrainer computes mean cross-entropy over label tokens, so label-only loss does not amplify the effective learning rate.\nHowever, this ablation simultaneously changes three variables: (1)~which tokens receive gradient updates, (2)~the implicit regularization from predicting trace tokens, and (3)~the training signal distribution.\nThe resulting improvement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} provides an \\emph{upper-bound estimate} of gradient dilution's contribution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects diagnostic signal dilution at inference, but the decomposition is approximate (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n",
